# Denetim Export — Parca 2 (hikaye 51-100 / 200)

## st-0122 — The Gift of the Magi
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
They live in a small room. They have little money. They love each other very much. They want to give a present. They keep the present away. They hope for a good day.

Della has a long head. She sells her head. She get money for the head. She buys a new watch present. The present is good for the watch. She put the present in the room. She hopes Jim like it.

Jim love his watch. He can not buy a present. He sells his watch. He get money for the watch. He buys a new head present. The present is good for the head. He put the present in the room. He hopes Della feel good.

Night comes and they sit together. They give each other the present. Della give Jim the watch present. Jim give Della the head present. They feel good. They see the watch present need watch. They see the head present need head. Both present now are not good. But love stay and is good. Morning comes and they feel good. They know love is the great present. Their room is small but love big. They hope to work and hold. They want to buy new present next year.

Friends ask how they can be good. They say love is enough. They help each other when need. Night end and they stay. They thank each other for love. Next day they walk hand in hand. Love will lead them through any problem. Love is the true present.

### A2
Della and Jim are a couple. They live in a house. It is the night before the new year.

Della has long hair. She wants to sell hair. She will get money for a present.

She goes to a shop. The shop will pay good money. She gets enough money.

She buys a new watch for Jim. She puts the present in a box. She feels good about the plan.

Jim loves the watch. He wants to get money for a present. He goes to a market.

The market will pay good money. He gets enough money. He buys a new hair present for Della.

He puts the present in a box. He feels good about the plan. He thinks Della will laugh.

That night they sit by a fire. They give each other a present. Both laugh and feel good.

Della opens Jim's present. It is a watch. Jim opens Della's present.

It is hair. Each present needs the thing they sell. Each present can not use now.

Both feel a bit bad. Both realize love is the best present. Both feel thank for each other.

Both know they sell much. Both thank each other. Love will grow.

Both plan to work hard. Both will save money for next year. Both want to buy new present.

Both are going to plan more present. House feels good. Both keep love each day.

Both will walk hand in hand. Love will lead both. The true present is love.

Both laugh in the morning. Both look at the present. Both say love is enough.

House feels good. Both keep love each day. Both will walk hand in hand.

Love will lead both. The true present is love. Both feel good and hope.

Both think about the next new year. Both will work together to earn more money. Both will share each task.

Both will avoid any more bad trade. Both will enjoy each moment of love. Family will hear about the good story soon.

Everyone will say love can change any hard thing. Love is the best present. Della and Jim are happy.

They live in a house. They have a good life. They love each other.

They will always be together. They will always love each other. They are a happy couple.

They have a good story. Their story is about love. Love is the best thing.

Della and Jim are a good example. They show that love is important. Love can change anything.

Della and Jim are happy now. They have a good life. They love each other.

They will always be happy. They will always love each other. They are a happy couple.

Love is the best present. Della and Jim know this. They will always love each other.

### B1
Della and Jim are a couple in a small house. It is the night before the new year. They have little money. Both want to give a gift to each other. They keep the plan secret. The mind is full of hope. If they have more money, they will buy many gifts. But they know love is the good present. They both hope the gift will bring joy. The small house feels warm with love. They think about the future with hope.

Della has long hair. She decides to cut the hair to get money. She goes to a shop that buys hair. She hopes to get a good price. The hair is cut and the money is kept for a chain. She buys a chain that will fit a watch. She puts the chain in a small box. She hopes Jim will like the gift. She feels happy to cut her hair for love. The shop man gives her a fair price for the hair. She feels the chain will make Jim smile.

Jim loves his watch. He feels sad that he can not buy a gift for Della. He decides to give the watch to a shop that buys watches. He gets money for a gift for hair. He finds a set of gifts for hair. He puts the gifts in a small box. He writes a note that says love and hope. He hopes Della will be happy. He feels happy to give his watch for love. The shop man gives him cash for the watch. He thinks the hair gifts will help Della feel nice.

On the night before the new year they stay by a light. They give each other gifts. They feel warm inside. Della gives Jim the chain. Jim gives Della the gifts for hair. Both smile. When they open the gifts they see the chain can not be used without the watch. Jim sees the hair gifts can not be used without hair. They feel a little sad but love is still good. the light makes the room feel warm. They talk and laugh as they open the gifts. Even though the gifts are good, the love stays.

They have given each other dear love. They know the gifts are not the real gift. They are grateful for the love they have. If they keep the own things, they would not feel such love. They know love will grow. The small house may be poor but the love inside is big. They plan to work hard and save for next year. They hope for a good future. They talk about how they keep the money. They promise to keep love strong. They feel the night has been a lesson.

In the morning they look at the chain and the gifts. They laugh and share joy. They know the great gift is the love they share each day. The house is small but the love is full. They hope to save for new gifts next year. They thank each other for the true gift. They feel safe and hope. They close the eyes and thank the night. They are ready for the next day. They think about the next year with hope. They plan to buy gifts when they have more money. They smile and hold each other close.

When friends ask how they can be so good, they say love is enough. They tell that love is more than money. They say any who needs help will get help. The story shows love is a strong force. Many people hear the story and feel hope. The lesson is clear: love is the good present. The night ends and the couple rests. They are ready for the next day. The love will guide them through any problem. Love never fades. The story ends with love. The story travels far and brings hope to many. People learn that love can beat any problem. The couple will keep love alive.

### B2
Della and Jim are both people who have little money on the night before the new year in a small house. Both want to give a new gift to each other because they love each other very. They keep the plan secret so the other will not know what the gift will be. The house is small but the hope in it is big. If they had more money, they would buy many gifts and share them with each other. They know that love can be the big gift even without many things, because love last.

Della, who has long hair, decide to sell hair to buy a new chain for Jim watch. She hoped to make Jim happy. She went to shop where hair was sold and money was kept for chain. She planned to put chain in a small box and hope Jim will love the gift.

Jim, who love his watch, feel bad that he can not buy a gift for Della hair. So he think of a plan. He decide to sell watch to get money for a gift for Della hair, hope to make Della good. The watch was sold at market and the money was kept well. If Jim had not sold watch, he would not have enough money for gift, and Della would be bad.

On the night before the new year they sit together by light and exchange gift and feel good. Della gives Jim the chain and Jim gives Della the gift for hair, both smile. When they open gift they see that chain can not be used without watch, which is a problem. Jim also see that gift for hair needs hair that is no longer there, make him feel bad. The gift are now not use but love behind them shine good, fill the room.

They see that the gift are not what matters but love that each show. The fact that each sold something show how much they care for each other, a good sign. If they had kept things, they would not have felt such great love, which now shine. The story shows that a good gift is love that they have in think, good and good.

In morning they look at chain and gift and smile, share a good feel. They know that the big gift is love they share each day, a same gift. The small house may be poor but love in it is big, good every place. They hope to work hard and save for new gift next year, planning a good future together. The love that they gave each other will always be the good gift, a gift that never stop.

When friends ask how they could be so good, they answer that love is enough for all. They tell that the measure of both is not money but care, a simple good. If anyone needed help, they would give what they can, even if it means a small loss, showing care. The story go like a good wind that show love can be the big power, move many think.

Many people hear and think about the gift and the love that it brings, feel hope rise. In the end the lesson is good: love is the good gift we can give, simple and good. The night stop and both rest, feel big in love and hope, ready for next. They close each eyes and thank each other for the good gift they share, feel good. The next day will rise and they will start a new day together, walking hand in hand. Love will lead them through any problem that may come, keep them big together. They know that big gift can never be bought, only give from think, good and free. And so the story stop with love shine good, a day that never stop.

They think that next year they will be able to buy a watch and a hair care set without selling anything. Their plan is to save a little each month and to spend money wisely on good gift. If they keep the habit of sharing love, they will always feel rich even if money is small. The group around them sees their love and may help them when they need help. Their story may inspire many other people who think love is the big gift. Thus the simple act of giving can change a whole town and bring hope. Even when night comes again, they will remember how love shine brighter than any chain or hair. They will always keep the memory of the first gift in a small box inside each home.

Every day they wake up and say thank for the love that fills the room. The light from the door shows a soft light that reminds them of the bright hope. If a rain comes, they will hold each other and know that love will protect them. Their friend may ask for help and they will give what they can, even if it means another small loss. Such kindness spreads like a gentle wind that carry the feel of love across the town. The town may become a place where each person feels safe and care. In this way the simple gift of love becomes a strong way that can change many lives. And that is the true gift that never ends. They celebrate each moment with joy.

### C1
The cramped apartment on the corner of the narrow street seemed to hold its breath as the last hours of the year slipped away. Only a single flickering lamp illuminated the modest furnishings, yet the air was charged with a hopeful warmth that no money could dim. Jim and Della, each with a pocket that barely clinked, had resolved to exchange gifts that would speak louder than any silver. Their love, though thinly veiled in poverty, shone brighter than the streetlights that struggled against the winter night. Both promised secrecy, fearing that the other's anticipation might be ruined by premature knowledge.

Della ran her fingers through her glossy, chestnut hair, feeling each strand as if it were a treasured relic. If she could afford only one gift, it would be the silver chain that would cradle Jim's beloved pocket watch. The thought of cutting away her hair seemed cruel, yet the image of Jim's smile made the sacrifice feel noble. She imagined the chain sliding over his wrist, its cold metal echoing the rhythm of their shared heartbeats. A quiet resolve settled in her chest; she would sell her hair, and no lingering doubt would stay.

At the bustling market, the scent of boiled cabbage and fresh bread mingled with the clatter of barters. She approached the modest stall where a kindly woman bought hair, offering a modest sum that would suffice for the chain. The woman's eyes softened as Della unfolded her tresses, and the transaction felt less like loss and more like an offering. With the coins jingling in her palm, Della entered the jeweler's shop, where a tiny silver chain glimmered on velvet. She slipped the chain into a petite box, whispering a promise to Jim that love could adorn even a humble wrist.

Jim stared at his aging pocket watch, its brass case scarred by years of sleepless nights and quiet joys. He knew the watch was his most prized possession, a heirloom that linked him to his late father. Yet the thought of Della's longing for a set of fine combs pressed upon his heart like a gentle ache. If he could surrender the watch, he would buy the combs that would celebrate her glorious hair, even if it meant a future baldness. The decision settled like a stone in his gut; love demanded a sacrifice, and he was ready to give.

The pawnshop clerk, a man with weathered hands, inspected the watch with reverence, as if handling a relic. He offered Jim a sum that would cover the cost of the delicate combs Della adored. As the coins clattered onto the counter, Jim felt the weight of his choice settle into his bones. He purchased the combs, their ivory shafts gleaming like promise, and wrapped them in soft tissue paper. The tiny parcel was placed beside the box of chain, each waiting silently for the other's eyes.

When midnight approached, the apartment filled with the faint scent of pine and the glow of a solitary candle. Della tucked the chain into her palm, feeling its cool weight against her trembling fingers. Jim cradled the wrapped combs, his heart beating faster than the ticking of his own watch. They sat across from each other, the candlelight casting soft shadows that danced like shy witnesses to their ceremony. With trembling hands they exchanged the parcels, each hoping the other would recognize the love hidden within.

Della unwrapped the small box, her eyes widening as the silver chain caught the candlelight. She lifted it, feeling the smooth metal glide over her palm, and imagined it encircling Jim's wrist. A sudden pang rose in her chest when she recalled that the watch, the reason for the chain, was no longer hers. She pressed the chain against her throat, the coolness a stark reminder of the sacrifice they had both made. Yet the tears that fell were not of sorrow, but of a fierce pride that love could command such price.

Jim opened his parcel, his fingers brushing the silky tissue before revealing the gleaming combs. He lifted one, admiring its ivory sheen, and pictured Della's hair catching the light like sunrise. A cold draft brushed his cheek as he remembered that his beloved watch, the reason for the combs, no longer ticked. The realization settled like a stone in his stomach, but his smile remained, warm and unshaken. He whispered a quiet thanks to the room, grateful for the depth of their shared devotion.

Both gifts, now rendered unusable, lay on the modest table as mute testimonies to their folly. Instead of remorse, a gentle laughter rose between them, filling the cramped space with a bright resonance. They clasped each other's hands, feeling the pulse of a love that needed no adornment. The night seemed to approve, the candle flame flickering in rhythm with their synchronized hearts. In that moment the poverty of their possessions vanished, replaced by a richness no market could ever buy.

Morning light filtered through the cracked window, casting golden bars across the floorboards where the chain and combs rested. They sat together, sipping tea that tasted of hope, and spoke of saving each penny for next year's gifts. Jim promised to mend the watch when he could afford a new spring, and Della vowed to grow her hair longer before selling again. Their conversation was peppered with laughter, each joke a brick in the foundation of a brighter future. Even the thin walls of their home seemed to echo with a newfound confidence that love could weather any storm.

When neighbors asked how they kept such bright spirits, Della and Jim answered simply: love is the greatest gift. Their story travelled through the village like a soft breeze, inspiring others to offer what they could, however small. Soon, the modest street became known for its generosity, each household sharing a piece of the love that Della and Jim had sparked. And so, each year when the clock struck midnight, the candle in their tiny home flickered brighter, a beacon of hope for all. For they had learned that a gift given from the heart never loses its shine, no matter how modest the wrapping.

## st-0123 — The Last Leaf
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
Johnsy is a new woman. She feels bad. She looks out the door. She sees many part on the wall. She says she will die. When last color fall. Johnsy stay in her room. She thinks about many part. She talks to Sue about part. She hopes part will not fall.

Sue is her friend. Sue feel bad. She tells Johnsy to stay good. She tells Johnsy to keep hope. Sue give food to Johnsy. Sue sit with Johnsy every. Sue say stay strong every day. Sue keep hope in room.

Behrman is an old man. He lives close to them. He hear talk. He feels need to help. Behrman think about Johnsy. He watch the part every night. He plans to act soon. He hopes act help.

A bad night come. Night is long and bad. Johnsy watch the part. She sees it move. The night is very bad. The part move in bad night. Johnsy feel bad fear.

In the morning light show. A single color stay on the wall. Johnsy see this and feel hope. Light good the room. Johnsy feel good hope. Sue see Johnsy look good.

Johnsy begin to get good. She looks good. Sue see her get good. Johnsy say more every day. Johnsy thank Sue. Johnsy thank Behrman.

They learn that Behrman go out. In bad night. He paint new color on the wall. He paint so Johnsy see a color. People hear about act. They say he is kind. They keep story. They call him great.

Behrman get bad. He die. Act keep Johnsy good. The town remember Behrman. The wall have many color. The part stay still.

### A2
Johnsy is a new woman. She lives in a small room. Johnsy feel very bad now. She feels very bad and sad. She lie in her small room. She can not work or play. She thinks the life is far. She asks Sue for help.

Johnsy look out the window. She sees a tree with many parts on the room. She says she will go when the last part falls. The tree is near the room. Many parts cover the tree. One part fall slow. Johnsy say the last part will fall. The time move the parts slow. Johnsy watch every move with good.

Sue is her friend. Sue feel very sad. Sue tell Johnsy she must stay good. Sue try to make Johnsy good. Sue give Johnsy food. Sue say good is in each day.

Behrman is an old person. He lives near them. He hear the news. He feels need to help. Behrman think about the part on the tree. He wants to make a new part. He plans to work at time.

A bad time come. The time is long and bad. Johnsy watch the part and see it move. The bad time is very long. Johnsy sit and watch the part. She feels very bad. The bad time make the room bad. Johnsy think about her friend.

Day shine. Day is good. A single part still stay on the tree. Day come again. The part still stay on the tree. Johnsy see the part and feel good. The day show the part good. Johnsy feel a new good.

Johnsy feel good now. She begin to get better. She smile more. Sue see her health improve. She eats small food each day. She talks with Sue each time. Her health get better each day. She reads a book each day. She plans to go away soon.

Later they learn Behrman go away in the bad time. He makes a new part on the tree. He feels very bad. He no longer live. But his act save Johnsy. He works hard in the bad time. He gives the part to Johnsy. He no longer live after the time. His act save Johnsy life. His name will live in each heart. People will talk about his act.

Johnsy live and feel good. Sue stay with her. Behrman be a good person. Johnsy live many more days. She thank Sue and remember Behrman. The part on the tree stay long.

### B1
Johnsy is a young woman who live in a small place. She has become sick and feel very weak. Johnsy has felt weak for many days. She looks out and see a tree on the wall. The tree have many parts on the wall. The parts are good and some are new. Johnsy say, "If the last part falls, I will die." She watch the parts and feel sad. She thinks about how each part may fall soon. Sue, her friend, is very worried. Sue tell Johnsy to stay strong and keep hope. Sue say, "You must try to get well and live." Johnsy listen and try to keep hope inside. She closes her eyes and picture a part staying on the tree. She feels a small change in her heart.

Behrman is an old man who love to draw. He lives near the small place and often walk there. He has many work on the wall. He hear about Johnsy and feel a need to help. Behrman think he can give a part to Johnsy. He says, "I will do something for her." He plans to stay awake and watch the tree at night. He hopes his work will give hope to Johnsy. He also think the part may be a sign for her. He feels a strong need to act soon. He tells his friend that he will try to draw a part. He goes to his small room and prepare draw.

A storm come and the night is long and cold. The wind blow hard and the rain fall fast. The tree shake and many parts move in the wind. Johnsy sit by the bed and watch the parts. She feels afraid but also keep hope inside. She thinks that each part may fall soon. The storm make the night seem endless. Sue stay with Johnsy and hold her hand. Sue say, "You must not give up." The cold make the room feel very bad. Johnsy close her eyes and try to rest. She hopes a part will stay on the tree.

In the morning the day is clear and warm. The day rise and the room get good. A single part still stay on the tree. Johnsy see the part and feel hope. She smile and feel her heart get warm. She begin to get better and eat more food. Sue watch Johnsy and feel good too. A friend come and say Johnsy health improve. Johnsy thank Sue and keep hope strong. The day go on and the wind stop. The tree still have the part on the wall. Johnsy feel that life may be good again.

Later they learn that Behrman went out in the storm. He goes into the night and walk to the wall. He draw a new part on the wall for Johnsy. He do this because he want Johnsy to see a part. He knows the part will give hope to Johnsy. The cold wind catch him and give him a cold. He feels very ill and cannot get well. Soon he die but his act save Johnsy. Johnsy hear the story and feel great thank. She knows that the part she see is not real but hope. The small place feel happy of Behrman. They keep the story and tell others about the act.

Johnsy keep hope and stay strong every day. Sue stay near and help Johnsy when needed. The tree still have many parts on the wall. The part that was draw stay there for all. Johnsy smile and think about life and hope. She knows that hope can keep a person alive. The story of Behrman become known in the small place. Everyone thank Behrman for his good act. The small place feel more kind and hope now.

### B2
Johnsy be a woman who live in a small home. She bed bad and feel very bad. She saw the door and see a Leaf on the wall. She said that she will die when the last Leaf be. Sue be a friend who feel very bad. She said that hope can make a big change. She said Johnsy that she must stay strong and keep hope.

Behrman be an old man who live by the wall. He hear the news and feel a need to help. He will make a new Leaf on the wall. A night be long and cold. Johnsy see the Leaf and see it move. She thought the Leaf may be bad. In the morning the day was bright and a single Leaf stay on the wall. Johnsy see the Leaf and feel hope. She begin to get well and feel good. Sue see the health improve and feel good. Later they learn that Behrman go out in the night and make a new Leaf on the wall. He do this so that Johnsy could see a Leaf and keep hope. Behrman get a cold from the night and be very bad. He die but his act help Johnsy. The story be about hope and love.

Each day Johnsy see the Leaf and feel strong. She said to Sue and share hope. Sue bring food and sit by the bed. They read a book and say about the future. The day be and the room be. Johnsy drink food and rest. Her health be better each night. The Leaf be still on the wall. When night was cold the Leaf not move. But the Leaf stay strong and give hope. If the Leaf go early Johnsy could feel bad. She said that she will not give up. Behrman, who be strong, see from the top. He saw the Leaf and smile. He thought his act be worth the risk. The cold be bad and the night be long. Johnsy sleep and dream of the Leaf. In the dream the Leaf be good and good. She get and feel good. Sue be good to see the change. The doctor come and say the health improve. He said that hope can help health. Johnsy thank Sue and thank Behrman. She said that love and hope be strong. The wall was old but the Leaf be new. The story be share by many. People hear the story and feel good. The point be that a small act can save a life. If a people give hope, a life could change. The Leaf was put by Behrman and stay.

When the season come, the wall be many Leaf. Johnsy see the new Leaf stay and feel good. She thought that the first Leaf may have help her. If the first Leaf not be there, she could not live. Behrman, who be old, die but his act live on. His name be talk by the town. The wall be many Leaf now. Each Leaf be a sign of hope. The people who pass by stop and look. They ask Sue why the Leaf stay. Sue say that love and work can make a Leaf stay. She also say that hope be the good strong. The story be yet hard. It be that a small act can change a big mind. If we give hope, we give life. The Leaf was put by a strong man and stay. It be a reminder that we all can help. The end be a new start for Johnsy. She planned to help others and keep hope. She will be kind and give hope. The town will keep and tell the story. And the Leaf will stay on the wall for many years.

Johnsy often sit by the wall and watch the Leaf. She talked to the Leaf as if it be a friend. The friend Sue bring tea and sit close. They share stories about the past and future. Johnsy feel that each day bring new hope. The town people hear the story and feel kind. A child ask why the Leaf stay good. Sue answer that love can keep a Leaf stay. The doctor visit often and check the health. He said that hope and rest help health. Johnsy thank the doctor and thank the Leaf. She thought that the Leaf be a present. The present give her help each night. When night was cold the Leaf not shake. But the Leaf stay good and give hope. If the Leaf not be there, the room was sad. Johnsy could feel lost without the Leaf. She said that she will find new hope. She planned to plant a new Leaf. The town help and bring water. Together they make a small place near the wall. There many Leaf stay on the place. Johnsy smile and feel that hope stay. The story go far and many hear. It be a part of the town life.

Johnsy write a note about the Leaf and hope. She gave the note to Sue and to the doctor. The note say that a small act can change a life. It also say that love and hope be strong. Sue read the note and feel good. She told the town that the Leaf be a sign. The town keep the story each year. They place a new Leaf on the wall as a act. The act remind all that hope never die. Even when night was cold, the Leaf stay good. The story stay in the mind of each person. And the hope will keep the town live.

### C1
Johnsy, a thin young woman confined to a cramped attic room, had been diagnosed with pneumonia that stubbornly refused to loosen its grip. Every morning she gazed out through the cracked window and fixed her eyes on the single ivy leaf clinging to the brick wall opposite her bed. When the wind whispered through the alley, the leaf trembled, and Johnsey whispered a desperate promise: when that leaf finally fell, she would join it in death. Her friends called this the 'last leaf' curse, and the notion settled over her like a cold, unrelenting fog.

Sue, her longtime confidante, entered the room each afternoon carrying a steaming cup of tea and a gentle smile. She reminded Johnsy that hope, however fragile, could shift the balance between life and death. 'You must cling to that leaf as if it were a lifeline,' Sue urged, her voice steady despite the gloom. She promised to stay by the bedside, to read aloud, and to fill the room with stories that lifted spirits.

Across the narrow lane lived Behrman, an elderly painter whose rough hands had once sketched grand cityscapes on canvas. He had long dreamed of creating a masterpiece, but his ambition had been eclipsed by poverty and the quiet resignation of old age. When Sue confided Johnsy's fatal prophecy, Behrman's weathered heart stirred with a sudden, unspoken purpose. He resolved to paint a single, evergreen leaf on the wall, hoping its permanence might deceive the fevered imagination of the sick girl.

One bitter night, the wind howled through the cracked shutters, and Behrman's thin candle flickered as he carried a brush and a tin of green paint. He climbed the rickety ladder, his joints protesting each step, and reached the spot where the solitary leaf clung to the brick. With careful strokes he traced the outline of a leaf, filling it with a vivid hue that seemed to glow against the night. The cold seeped through his coat, and as he painted, a shiver ran through his spine, warning him of the storm's ruthless bite.

At dawn, the first pale light slipped through the window, revealing a single, bright leaf that had not fallen. Johnsy stared at the stubborn green shape, her breath catching as she wondered whether the leaf had somehow defied nature. A sudden surge of hope rose within her, warm and bright, as if the leaf itself whispered, 'Stay.' She felt a faint tickle of strength in her chest, and for the first time in weeks she managed to sit upright without pain. Sue entered quietly, her eyes widening at the unchanged leaf, and she clasped Johnsy's hand, silently sharing the newly kindled optimism.

Day by day, Johnsy's color returned; her cheeks flushed pink, and the cough that had rattled her nights grew softer. Sue brought fresh broth, read aloud from adventure tales, and occasionally teased Johnsy about the leaf's stubbornness. The room, once cold and silent, filled with the scent of rosemary and the soft murmur of hopeful conversation. Even the small cracked window seemed less oppressive, as if the wind itself were holding its breath out of respect. Johnsy began to laugh at the sound of her own voice, a sound that had been absent for far too long.

When the town doctor visited, he noted the steady rise in her temperature and the brightening of her eyes. He praised Sue's diligent care but added, 'Your spirit's renewed hope is the most potent medicine I have ever prescribed.' Johnsy thanked him, feeling a deep gratitude that extended beyond her recovery to the very people who had nurtured her will. She whispered a quiet promise to the leaf, vowing that if it had saved her, she would someday repay its silent courage.

Later that week, the townsfolk learned that Behrman had braved the freezing night to paint the leaf, catching a lethal chill. His pneumonia, aggravated by the damp air, worsened rapidly, and within days his frail body could no longer sustain life. When Sue heard the news, tears fell silently, but she also felt a profound gratitude for his self-sacrifice. Behrman's final brushstroke, though simple, had become a beacon that illuminated the narrow alley with unexpected resilience. His death, though tragic, proved that a single act of love could alter the course of another's destiny.

Inspired by Behrman's sacrifice, Johnsey resolved to devote herself to caring for others who teetered on the brink of despair. She began to write letters of encouragement, sending them to patients in distant hospitals, each containing a tiny sketch of a leaf. Sue helped distribute the notes, and together they witnessed dozens of recoveries that seemed to echo the original miracle. The two women often met beneath the same wall, now adorned with a chorus of painted leaves, each a reminder of perseverance.

Visitors to the neighborhood soon asked Sue why the leaf on the wall never fell, and she answered with quiet certainty. She explained that love and steadfast effort could suspend even the harshest of natural cycles, at least in the hearts of those who believed. The doctor, now a frequent guest, affirmed that patients who felt supported often recovered faster, confirming the power of communal hope. The wall, once plain and weathered, became a living tapestry, each leaf a testament to a story that the town cherished.

One spring afternoon, Johnsy penned a short note describing how a single painted leaf had sparked her recovery and urged others to act. She handed the paper to Sue and the doctor, who placed it on a small wooden plaque beside the mural. The plaque read, 'A small act of love can keep hope alive,' and it quickly became a focal point for newcomers. Every year, the community gathered to paint a fresh leaf, renewing the promise that compassion never withers.

Now, as autumn winds rustle the new leaves overhead, the original green leaf still clings stubbornly to the wall, defying time. Johnsy, healthier and brighter, walks past it each day, nodding gratefully, knowing that the simple act of one old painter saved her life. She continues to tend to the sick, offering the same leaf as a quiet symbol of perseverance. And the town, remembering Behrman's quiet heroism, keeps the story alive, allowing each passerby to glimpse the power of a single, steadfast leaf.

### B2
In New York two close friends made a promise that they would meet again at the same place after twenty years. They shook hands and said the promise was a bond that would never fade. Each friend went on own way and never forgot the old promise. Time passed and the years grew long, but the thought of the meeting stayed bright. On the appointed night a dark shop door stood still, waiting for the two old friends. The city lights flickered above the shop as the clock struck the early hour of the night.

A man waited in the dark shop door, his heart beating fast as he looked at the empty street. A passing man came by, and the waiting man shouted a loud hello, hoping his old friend would hear. The waiting man told the passing man about his hard life out west, where he had faced many danger and had succeeded. He said the promise was still good for him, and he would wait until the night turned to morning. He stood there quietly, feeling the cold on his face as the city lights glowed far away. The shop door creaked, and the waiting man held his breath, waiting for the moment.

Soon another man arrived, his steps steady, and he called out a greeting that sounded familiar. The waiting man looked at the new man and felt something was not right, a sense of problem. He thought the man might not be his old friend, and his feel beat faster as time passed. If the man was his friend, he would smile and hug his old companion. The waiting man was told that the new man had a secret that could change the night. He realized the newcomer was not his old friend, but a man from his past life.

The new man was Jimmy, a man who had become a city man after many years. Jimmy had known the waiting man since they were boys, and they had shared many years. Jimmy could not bear to take his old friend away, for the city was strict. Instead he sent another man to do the work, and he told the waiting man why he could not stay. He said the promise was still good, but the time had changed and the night could not bring them together. The waiting man listened, felt sad, and understood that the old bond would live on in his mind.

The waiting man thanked Jimmy, turned away, and walked out into the dark night. He felt the cold on his face as the city lights glowed far away. He thought about the old promise and the many years that had passed since their last meeting. If he had known the night would end this way, he might have chosen another path. He walked far into the city, looking for a new life that could bring hope. The thought of his friend stayed with him for the rest of his life, a quiet comfort.

He remembered the walks they had taken as boys, the games they played, and the plans they made in the early dawn. Each memory felt like a warm light that could guide him through the dark night of the city, of his childhood. He thought that maybe the promise was not a chain but a bridge that could link past and future. If he could find a new purpose, he might turn his hard past into a good future for himself. He decided that the night would not end his story, but would start a new chapter full of hope. He walked toward the bright street, feeling the pulse of the city and the rhythm of his mind.

Another man appeared behind the shop on the corner, his eyes scanning the quiet street. He was sent by Jimmy to watch the waiting man and to keep the promise safe. The other man nodded, understood the task, and moved toward the waiting man with quiet steps. He could not speak, but his eyes showed that he was ready to do his duty. If the night would grow darker, the other man would stay until the promise was fulfilled or the dawn arrived. The city seemed to hold its breath, waiting for the final moment that would decide the fate of the two men.

At last the first light of dawn painted the sky, and the waiting man felt a new beginning. He turned his back to the shop door, left the past behind, and walked toward the rising sun. Jimmy watched from afar, his mind heavy but his duty done, and he smiled at the quiet street. The promise remained in the thought of both men, a silent link that could not be broken. If one day they should meet again, they would greet each other with a warm smile and a firm hand. Until that day, the story of the night would live on in the quiet corners of the city.

The city never forgets the small acts of loyalty that shape its many lives. Each night brings new chances for old promises to be kept, even if the paths have changed. The quiet street still holds the echo of a promise spoken long ago beneath a dark shop door. And in that echo, the two friends find a small comfort that time cannot erase. Their story reminds us that loyalty may survive even the longest separation and the darkest night. So the promise after twenty years lives on, a quiet fire that never truly fades.

### C1
On a rain-slick November evening in New York, two boys of the same street pledged to meet again beneath the same shop door after twenty years. They clasped hands tightly, feeling the rough leather of each other's palms as a silent oath settled between them. The promise, they swore, would never fade like the city's neon, but remain as solid as the iron bolts holding the door. With a shared grin and a flicker of hope, they went their separate ways, each carrying the invisible thread of that promise.

Years stretched into decades, and the city grew taller, its streets humming with countless strangers who never heard the boys' secret vow. Yet, each night before sleep, each remembered the crack in the wooden door where they had sealed their pact. The thought of the reunion glowed brighter than any lantern that ever lit the alleys of their youth. When the appointed night finally arrived, a thin fog curled around the shop, and the streetlamps cast trembling halos on the wet pavement. The clock on the distant tower struck twelve, its deep chime echoing like a summons to both souls.

At the threshold, a man in a long overcoat stood motionless, his breath forming small clouds that vanished into the night air. His heart hammered against his ribs, each thud a reminder that time was slipping away like sand through fingers. When a passerby hurried past, he raised his voice, shouting a hopeful 'Hello!' into the empty street, hoping his old companion might hear. The wind carried the cry, but the darkness returned unanswered, as if the night itself were holding its breath. He thought of the rugged frontier he had left behind, of scorching suns and rattling coyotes, and of the promise that still bound him.

A stranger paused, curiosity flickering in his eyes, and the waiting man seized the moment to recount his hard-won tales. He spoke of scorching deserts, sudden sandstorms, and gunfire that cracked the silence of lonely canyons. Each triumph he described-finding water after days of thirst, outwitting bandits, earning the respect of hardened miners-was a thread that stitched the past to the present. He reminded himself that the promise was still as real as the compass that guided him through endless plains. With a solemn nod, he declared he would wait until the darkness yielded to dawn, for the bond could not be broken by distance.

A sudden creak disturbed the silence as the shop door shivered on its hinges, and the man inhaled sharply, feeling the night tighten around him. He pressed his palm against the cold wood, as though hoping the timber might convey a hidden signal to his long-lost friend. Minutes stretched, each tick of a distant clock sounding like a heart-beat in the gloom, and his patience grew thin as paper. Yet the street remained empty, the shadows unmoving, and the promise seemed to hang precariously in the stale air. He steadied his breathing, recalling the exact moment of the handshake, and whispered to the night that he would not betray it.

At last, footsteps resonated on the cobblestones, and a figure emerged, his coat trimming the fog like a dark brushstroke. He called out a greeting that trembled with familiarity, and the waiting man's eyes flickered, searching for the face he remembered. A cold knot tightened in his stomach; something about the newcomer's gait, the angle of his hat, felt off, like a mis-tuned chord. He wondered whether the stranger was truly his childhood companion or merely a phantom conjured by yearning. If the man were his friend, a smile would surely blossom, and an embrace would dissolve the years like sugar in tea. Instead, a subtle tension pulsed between them, as if an unseen barrier guarded the past.

The newcomer stepped forward, and the waiting man recognized him-not as the boy from his memories, but as Jimmy, now a city gentleman. Jimmy had risen from the same dusty alleys, trading his spurs for polished shoes, and he now moved within the bustling avenues of commerce. He confessed that the city's strict codes barred him from keeping the old promise in its original form, and he had dispatched a proxy instead. The proxy, a silent sentinel, would stand watch until dawn, ensuring that the bond would not be shattered by the passage of time. Jimmy's eyes, though softened by nostalgia, hardened when he explained that his obligations to the city left him no room for personal reunions. The waiting man listened, felt a pang of sorrow, yet understood that the promise lived on in memory, not in the flesh.

He offered Jimmy a firm handshake, the grip still echoing the strength of their youthful pact, and whispered gratitude. Then, turning away from the shop's dim doorway, he stepped onto the slick pavement, feeling the night's chill seep into his bones. He recalled the games they had played as children-hide-and-seek among the cracked bricks, daring each other to touch the moonlit gutters. Each memory glowed like a lantern in his mind, guiding him through the darkness that now stretched ahead like an uncharted river. He resolved that the promise was not a chain imprisoning him, but a bridge linking past to future, urging him onward. With each step, the city's pulse matched his own, and the distant hum of early traffic whispered of new possibilities.

Behind the shop, a figure emerged from the shadows, his eyes scanning the quiet street with vigilant intensity. He was the man Jimmy had sent, a silent guardian tasked with preserving the integrity of the old vow. Without uttering a word, he slipped toward the waiting man, his movements as smooth as a cat's tread. His presence was a quiet reassurance that the promise would not dissolve into oblivion, even as dawn threatened to erase the night. The city seemed to hold its breath, the flickering streetlights casting long shadows that stretched like questions unanswered.

When the first pale ribbons of sunrise brushed the horizon, the waiting man felt a sudden surge of resolve. He turned his back on the shop door, leaving behind the phantom of the past, and walked toward the light. Jimmy, standing a short distance away, watched the silhouette recede, a faint smile playing across his lips despite the weight of duty. The silent sentinel remained, his gaze fixed on the empty doorway, ready to linger until the city awoke fully. In that moment, the promise-though unfulfilled in flesh-radiated a quiet assurance that memory could outlast any separation.

The city, ever a mosaic of countless vows, never forgets the small acts of fidelity that stitch its narrative together. Each night, as streetlamps flicker and the wind sighs through alleys, echoes of promises whispered long ago linger in the air. For the two friends, the bond remains a quiet fire, a steady glow that neither time nor distance can extinguish. And so, after twenty years, their story endures in the corners of the city, a reminder that loyalty can survive the darkest of nights.

### C1
It was a bitter December night when a shivering stranger entered 221B Baker Street, clutching a tattered hat and a plump goose. His breath formed quick clouds, and his eyes darted furtively as he placed the awkward bundle on the cluttered table. Holmes rose from his armchair, his keen gaze falling immediately upon the hat, its brim frayed like old rope. The goose, bewildered, hopped onto the polished surface, its feathers rustling like distant whispers. A faint, metallic glint caught Holmes's eye as the bird shifted, revealing a small, blue gemstone lodged within its cavity.

The stone was unmistakably a carbuncle, its sapphire hue deepening the mystery that had already begun to coil around the case. Holmes recognized instantly that the gem had been stolen from a wealthy mansion the previous week, a theft that had baffled Scotland Yard. With the holiday season approaching, he felt an urgent need to resolve the matter before the city's festive cheer was soured. He ordered a fresh pot of tea, the steam curling like ghostly ribbons, while his mind traced the possible routes the goose might have taken. The faint scent of roasted chestnuts drifted from the street below, reminding him that time was slipping like sand through an hourglass.

Following the faint imprint of feathers, Holmes stepped out into the fog-laden alley, the night air sharp and tinged with coal smoke. He paused before the bustling market square, where stalls glowed amber and the clamor of haggling voices rose like a restless tide. A vendor, his apron smeared with flour, greeted him with a wary smile, his eyes flicking to the goose perched on the counter. Holmes asked where the bird had been purchased, and the shopkeeper recalled a hurried transaction with a man clutching a scarlet hat. The merchant added that the buyer had paid a pittance, his hands trembling as if the weight of a secret pressed upon them.

The shopkeeper's recollection led Holmes to a narrow lane where a lone trader, his coat patched but his demeanor confident, sold curiosities to passersby. When questioned, the trader described receiving the goose from a gaunt figure emerging from a distant, poorly lit street. That figure, he said, had offered the bird as a present for his child, insisting the animal was harmless and full of cheer. Holmes noted the peculiar insistence, for a simple goose rarely traveled such distances without a purpose beyond mere amusement. The trader's description of the scarlet hat matched the one described by the market vendor, tightening the thread that bound the mystery together.

Guided by this chain of testimony, Holmes ventured toward the quiet backstreet where the gaunt figure had supposedly dwelt. The street was silent, save for the occasional clatter of a carriage wheel and the soft sigh of a distant church bell. A narrow doorway stood ajar, its wooden panels warped by years of winter wind, inviting Holmes to peek inside. Inside, a diminutive man hunched over a low table, his fingers trembling around a glinting blue gem. His face, illuminated by a flickering candle, revealed a mix of fear and reluctant relief as he sensed Holmes's presence.

Holmes stepped forward, his silhouette cutting the wavering light, and spoke in a calm tone that seemed to steady the room. He produced the blue carbuncle from the goose's hollow, its fire-red sparkle reflected in the man's wide eyes. The thief's confession slipped out without a word; he had taken the gem from the Countess's study, hoping the bird would hide it. Holmes observed that the man's trembling hands were not the result of greed alone, but of a desperate yearning for redemption. Instead of demanding punishment, he offered the man a chance to return the gem anonymously, trusting the spirit of the season to inspire change.

The man, eyes brimming with tears, nodded gratefully, his shoulders easing as if a weight had been lifted. He slipped the carbuncle into his coat, promising to deposit it with the police after midnight, when no one could trace its path. Holmes, satisfied with the man's resolve, reminded him that the true treasure lay not in jewels but in the goodwill fostered by the holidays. He turned toward the open street, the cold wind brushing his coat, and felt a rare sense of quiet triumph. The goose, still perched on the table, seemed to puff its feathers in contentment, as if aware of its role in the resolution.

Back at Baker Street, Holmes placed the blue carbuncle into a drawer, its crimson glow dimming under the lamplight. He poured himself a cup of tea, the steam curling upward, and reflected on how a small bird had unraveled a grand theft. The case reminded him that even the most intricate puzzles could hinge upon a single, seemingly insignificant detail. He scribbled a brief note in his diary, emphasizing the value of compassion over retribution during festive times. A soft knock at the door announced the return of the market boy, who handed Holmes a tiny trinket shaped like a goose.

The boy explained that his mother had asked him to sell a similar bird, believing it would bring luck to its owner. Holmes smiled, noting how ordinary commerce could intersect with extraordinary events, weaving a tapestry of chance and intention. He placed the tiny goose beside the real one, a reminder that the smallest objects often carry the greatest stories. The market boy bowed respectfully and left, his footsteps echoing the soft rhythm of the snowy streets outside. Outside, a gentle snow began to fall, muffling the city's clamor and sealing the night in serene stillness.

News of the recovered carbuncle spread quickly through the neighborhood, and children whispered about the clever detective and the brave goose. The Countess, upon hearing of the gem's return, sent a handwritten note thanking Holmes for his discretion and kindness. She praised his decision to spare the thief, arguing that mercy during Yuletide could inspire redemption in even the darkest hearts. Holmes, reading the elegant script, felt a quiet pride that his intellect had served not only justice, but also humanity. He resolved to keep the case as a lesson, reminding himself that every clue, however modest, might change a destiny.

As the new year dawned, the city awoke to a quiet optimism, its streets glistening beneath a pale winter sun. Holmes stood at his window, watching the light play upon the rooftops, and felt a rare contentment settle within him. He lifted the tiny goose, placed it beside his teacup, and whispered a quiet promise to protect such fragile miracles. The case file, now closed, rested among his other chronicles, a testament to the power of empathy interlaced with deduction. And so, the legend of the blue carbuncle and the humble goose would be retold each winter, a gentle reminder of redemption's quiet flame.

### C1
Jabez Wilson was a man of singular appearance, his fiery red hair marking him out in any crowd. He managed a small pawnbroking shop that sat nestled closely against the bustling heart of the financial district. Behind his counter, he maintained a world of meticulous order, polishing every surface until it gleamed with satisfaction. His business dealt in food provisions and small trinkets, serving a steady stream of neighborhood regulars. Wilson found deep satisfaction in the routine cleanliness and bright atmosphere of his modest establishment. It was a simple life, but one he guarded with pride and diligent attention.

One afternoon, the celebrated detective Sherlock Holmes stepped into the shop, his gaze sweeping the room with analytical precision. He had been passing by when he noticed Wilson working and decided to inquire about the man's recent fortunes. Holmes asked directly if Wilson had encountered any peculiar opportunities suited to a man with red hair. Wilson confirmed that a strange employment offer had indeed come his way recently. He recounted the details of a position that seemed both lucrative and oddly specific. There was a sharpness in Holmes's demeanor that suggested he was not merely making casual conversation.

The job required Wilson to copy the entire Encyclopaedia Britannica by hand for a mysterious organization called the Red-Headed League. He was to work in a secluded office for several hours each day, transcribing the text without error. The pay was remarkably generous for such simple labor, yet the position was strictly temporary. Wilson confessed that he often felt the role might be a trick to keep him away from his business. The rules were strict, demanding absolute silence and concentration, which Wilson had followed with obedience. Holmes nodded gravely, for he had already deduced the likely motive behind this peculiar arrangement.

Holmes explained his theory with a logic that left no room for doubt in Wilson's mind. He believed the fraudulent job served only one purpose: to remove the shopkeeper from his premises daily. While Wilson was occupied elsewhere, an accomplice was utilizing the basement for a far more sinister task. Holmes postulated that a tunnel was being excavated from the shop's cellar toward the adjacent bank. Such a passage would allow thieves to bypass the vault's formidable defenses and steal the gold within. It was a classic diversion, utilizing human vanity to distract the victim from the reality unfolding beneath his feet.

Realizing the gravity of the threat, Holmes insisted they act immediately to thwart the impending robbery. He contacted the police and the bank director, proposing a coordinated stakeout for the coming night. They would lie in wait inside the darkened vault, listening for the first sign of an intrusion. Holmes himself would take up a silent position within the empty shop, watching over the basement door. The city outside continued its noisy life, entirely unaware of the trap being set. The plan was simple in its execution but required nerves of steel to see it through to the end.

The police officers and bank staff settled into the deepest shadows of the vault, their eyes straining against the gloom. A bright electric lamp had been rigged near the expected breach point, ready to flood the area with light. A camera, a modern marvel of evidence gathering, was positioned to capture the criminal in the act. Silence enveloped them, heavy and suffocating, as they waited for the inevitable moment. Holmes, crouched in the darkness of the shop, watched the street with the patience of a predator. They were hunting a shadow, a criminal who believed himself to be the master of his own fate.

As the night deepened, a figure emerged stealthily into the shop's quiet basement, moving with practiced caution. The intruder carried tools hidden beneath his coat, intending to break through the final barrier of the tunnel. He began to dig, his movements rhythmic and deliberate, widening the hole toward the bank's vault. Suddenly, the rigged lamp flared, blinding him in the sudden brilliance, while the camera clicked softly in the background. The sound of digging ceased instantly, replaced by the scuffling of panic and confusion. He had calculated every move, but he had not calculated on the presence of the world's greatest detective.

The officers surrounded the thief just as he broke through the final layer of earth into the vault room. He froze, his hands hovering over the stacks of money he had intended to steal for months. Realization dawned on his face that his meticulous plan had been discovered and completely dismantled. The police officers were efficient and professional, denying him even a moment of hope for escape. The hole, now exposed to the harsh light, revealed the full extent of his criminal ambition. It was a physical manifestation of greed, now lying in ruins before the eyes of the law.

Back at the bank, the thief attempted to justify his actions, claiming he was merely a tool for a secret society. Holmes dismantled this lie, explaining that the Red-Headed League was nothing but an elaborate fabrication. Its sole purpose had been to manipulate Wilson and provide the necessary cover for the tunnel's construction. The bank director shook Holmes's hand warmly, acknowledging that the detective's insight had saved them a fortune. Wilson, having returned to his shop, felt a wave of relief wash over him. His world, which had been threatened by unseen forces, was secure once more.

Wilson thanked Holmes profusely, vowing to be far more vigilant in the future regarding offers that seemed too good to be true. The shop would reopen the next day, serving the community with the same dedication as before. Holmes returned to his home, his mind already turning toward the next puzzle that awaited him in the vast city. He reflected on how easily ordinary life could be disrupted by criminal ingenuity and greed. Yet, he knew that truth had a way of prevailing over deceit in the end. Holmes knew that the city held many such secrets, and he would always be there to uncover them.

## st-0127 — The Ant and the Grasshopper
Kaynak: PD-uyarlama

### A1
All day long, a small little thing plays. He plays in the light. The small little thing likes the game. A hard worker brings food. He keeps food for the late year. The worker moves food. He brings bits of food home. He knows hard days will come soon. He wants to be ready for the cold time. He does not stop to play.

The small little thing looks at the hard worker. He looks at him. He asks why the worker works hard. It is a good day. The small little thing tells him to play. They should just play together. The worker keeps working. He does not mind the small little thing. He has no time to play games now.

Soon, the time ends. The hard days come with white water. White water is out. The small little thing has no food left. He is very low on food. He is in great trouble. The worker is well in his house. He has a lot of food to eat. He stays warm inside his room.

The small little thing learns an important thing. He has a difficult time now. He sees something new. There is a time for work. There is a time for play. It is good to keep food. Hard days begin later. If he worked, he is not low on food now. He must work when the sun is up.

### A2
All summer long, a small animal sings in the hot day. He plays games every single day of the period. The little animal sings a song in the nice air. He does not want to work at all. He just wants to play music and have fun. He feels happy during the long summer months. The hot day is good for the little animal. He can play all day and night without a problem. Life is simple and free for him right now. He does not think about the future or food. He thinks tomorrow will be just like today.

At the same time, a hard worker animal carries food home. The animal stores food for the cold season. He knows cold days will come soon. The animal walks back and forth with much food. He makes his house safe and full of food. The animal works hard from morning until evening. He does not stop to play or sing a song. The animal knows he must prepare for the bad day. He wants to be safe when the cold air arrives. Every single day, the animal does his job well. He brings food into his small home.

The little animal looks at the busy animal. He laughs at the animal and asks why he works so hard. The animal tells him to sing and play instead. The animal says that working is a bad way to live. He wants the animal to join him in the fun. However, the animal keeps working all the time. The animal looks at the animal and shakes his head. He tells the animal to work for the future. The animal just smiles and avoids the hard worker completely. He thinks the animal is losing his valuable time. The animal goes back to his games and songs.

Soon, the bright summer ends completely. Cold season comes with white stuff and hard water. The little animal has no food left in his home. He is very cold and very bad now. The animal looks for food across the low ground. He finds nothing to eat in the cold air. The animal is nice inside his house. He has a lot of food to eat every day. The animal sits by the fire and feels very good. He is safe from the cold white stuff and water. The animal made a good choice during the summer.

The little animal learns an important lesson from this experience. He realizes that there is a time for play. He also knows there is a time for hard work. It is smart to prepare for the future early. If he had worked, he would not be bad now. The animal is very sorry for his past actions. He knows he must change his ways next year. The cold season teaches him a hard truth. Next summer, the animal is going to work hard too. He will store food before the cold white stuff comes. That is the end of the story for the animal.

### B1
All summer long, a small jumper sings and plays in the warm sun. While the jumper enjoys his game, a hard worker carries food. He carries food to store for the winter season. The worker spends every day moving bits of plant back home. He knows that cold days will arrive soon. He has worked hard because he wants to be safe. If he works all day, he will have plenty of food. He has collected many small plant pieces for his family. Every single day is important for his future safety. The warm sun is bright upon the green fields. Many small animals enjoy the beautiful weather while it lasts. The days pass quickly as summer reaches its high point. Every moment is filled with bright light and love. Nobody thinks about the cold days that will come later.

The jumper looks at the busy worker and laughs. He asks why the worker is working so hard on such a fine day. The jumper tells him that they should just sing and play instead. However, the worker keeps working and ignores the animal. The jumper does not understand why anyone would spend time like that. He believes that life is simply meant for love and song. The worker says nothing and continues moving his plant pieces. He knows that songs cannot feed anyone when snow falls. The jumper jumps around and sings another happy song. He feels completely free under the warm blue sky. Nothing can stop him from enjoying his favorite game. The bright hours pass by in a wonderful way. He has spent all his time in total peace.

Soon, the bright summer ends and the cold winter comes with snow and ice. The jumper has no food left anywhere. He is very weak and cold, and he finds himself in great trouble. Meanwhile, the worker is warm inside his house with plenty of food to eat. The white snow covers every part of the ground outside. Ice forms over the little water lines where water used to flow. The jumper shakes in the cold air because he has no home. He looks around for something to eat, but he finds nothing. His little legs feel tired and weak from the freezing wind. He wishes that he had listened to the worker before. If he had planned ahead, he would be safe now. The heavy snow continues to fall all through the long night. He realizes that his past choices were very bad.

The jumper learns an important lesson from this difficult experience. He realizes that there is a time for work and a time for play. It is good to plan for the future before hard times begin. If he had worked during the summer, he would not be weak now. He understands that fun cannot replace the need for food. The worker has shown him how to live through difficult seasons. He promises to change his ways when the next year begins. He will remember this cold winter for the rest of his life. Planning is the key to surviving the bitter ice. He closes his eyes and hopes for a better tomorrow. The bad season finally teaches him the true value of effort. He will never waste another warm summer day again. From now on, work will always come before play.

### B2
All summer long, a small animal sings and plays in the warm air. While the creature enjoys his game, a hard worker carries food to store for the winter season. The man spends every day moving bits of grain back home. He knows that cold days will arrive soon. This process happens every single year without fail. The worker believes that preparation is important for survival. He understands that difficult times will come when the weather changes. Therefore, he continues to work hard every morning and evening. His family supports him in this important task. Together, they collect enough food to ensure their safety during the dark months. Every member of the group contributes to the shared goal. They know that being lazy can lead to severe problems later in life. If the worker had not prepared, his family would suffer greatly. When the warm sun shines, the diligent worker is always seen gathering supplies. He believes that a bright future is built by careful planning. Many other creatures watch him move heavy loads across the field. However, they prefer to spend their hours in endless play. The worker knows that winter will bring snow and ice to the land. He understands that food will be difficult to find when the weather turns bad. Therefore, he ignores the songs of the little animal and keeps moving. Every piece of grain that is collected represents a step toward safety. The family helps him organize the store room with great care. They ensure that every item is placed in the correct position. Nothing is left to chance because survival depends on these actions. The community respects the worker for his dedication and effort.

The little animal looks at the busy worker and laughs. He asks why the worker is spending so much time on such a fine day. The animal tells him that they should just sing and play instead. However, the worker keeps moving and ignores the other person. The animal thinks that life is meant for joy and simple rest. He cannot see why anyone would spend bright days carrying heavy loads. So, he continues to dance and make music under the clear sky. He believes that tomorrow will take care of itself. Many other animals in the field also join the game. They enjoy the warm weather and forget about the future entirely. None of them want to think about the cold season that waits ahead. If they listened to the worker, they would start gathering food immediately. Instead, they waste precious hours on silly games and songs. The little animal tells his friends that worrying is a complete waste of time. He points to the sky and asks why anyone would be sad on such a lovely day. His friends agree with him and laugh loudly. They believe that food will somehow appear when they need it most. The worker watches them from a distance and feels sorry for their lack of vision. He knows that nature is harsh and unforgiving to those who do not prepare. Yet, the playful creatures continue their daily dance without a single care. The bright summer days pass by very quickly for everyone in the field. Soon, the warm breeze is replaced by a chilly wind. The signs of autumn become clear as leaves fall from the tall trees.

Soon, the bright summer ends and the cold winter comes with snow and ice. The small animal has no food left anywhere. He is very weak and cold, and he finds himself in great trouble. Meanwhile, the worker is warm inside his house with plenty of food to eat. The snow covers the ground, and the wind blows hard against the doors. The animal realizes that he made a terrible mistake by ignoring the warning signs. His friends are also suffering from the lack of food and shelter. They wander through the cold white landscape and look for something to eat. Every place they visit is empty and silent. The situation becomes more serious with each passing hour. They wish that they had listened to the advice of the wise worker earlier. If the animal had worked during the summer, he would be eating warm food now. Instead, he shivers in the freezing air and regrets his past choices. His empty stomach causes him great pain as the night grows darker. The snow falls heavily, covering all the paths they used to walk upon. They try to find berries or seeds beneath the thick ice, but nothing can be found. Their weak bodies struggle to move through the deep drifts. The little animal realizes that his playful attitude was entirely wrong. He remembers how he laughed at the worker who was busy storing grain. Now, that laughter turns into bitter tears of regret. The cold wind cuts through their thin fur, making them feel even worse. They hallow together under a large rock to escape the worst of the storm. Even in this dark moment, they receive no help from the frozen world around them.

The animal learns an important lesson from this difficult experience. He realizes that there is a time for work and a time for play. It is wise to prepare for the future before hard times begin. If he had worked during the summer, he would not be weak now. He finally understands the value of responsibility and proper planning. The experience changes his view on life completely. He promises himself that he will never make the same mistake again. When the next year comes, he will be ready to work just like the others. Hard work and preparation are essential for a good life. Everyone must learn this truth if they want to survive the winter. The worker hears a knock on his door and opens it slowly. He sees the cold and hungry animals standing outside in the snow. Although he is disappointed by their past behavior, he feels pity for them. He invites them inside his warm house and shares his food with them. The little animal thanks the kind worker with all his heart. He promises that he will never waste another summer on foolish games. When spring returns, the animal keeps his promise and starts working early. He helps his friends gather supplies for the next cold season. Together, they build a strong community based on hard work and mutual support. Every member contributes to the common goal of survival. The lessons of the past are never forgotten by anyone in the field. They now know that proper planning is the true key to happiness. Life becomes much better when everyone does their share of the work.

### C1
All summer long, a small grasshopper sings and plays in the warm, golden air. While the creature enjoys his endless game, a hard worker carries food to store for the winter season. The ant spends every day moving bits of grain back home, knowing that cold days will arrive soon. This process happens every single year without fail, anchored in the belief that preparation is vital for survival. He understands that difficult times will come when the weather changes, so he continues his relentless labor every morning and evening.

His family supports him in this important task, and together they collect enough food to ensure their safety during the dark months. Every member of the group contributes to the shared goal, fully aware that being lazy can lead to severe problems later in life. Had the worker not prepared diligently, his family would surely suffer greatly when the frost sets in. When the warm sun shines brightly, the industrious worker is always seen gathering heavy supplies across the field. He truly believes that a secure future is built by careful planning rather than passing time in idle amusement.

Many other creatures watch him move heavy loads, yet they prefer to spend their hours in endless play. The worker knows that winter will eventually bring snow and ice to the land, rendering food impossible to find. Therefore, he ignores the merry songs of the little grasshopper and keeps moving forward with quiet determination. Every piece of grain that is collected represents a tangible step toward safety and peace of mind. The family helps him organize the store room with great care, ensuring that every item is placed in the correct position without leaving anything to chance.

The little grasshopper looks at the busy worker and laughs openly at his unending toil. He asks why the worker is spending so much time on such a fine day, suggesting they should sing and play instead. However, the worker keeps moving and deliberately ignores the carefree artist. The grasshopper thinks that life is meant entirely for joy and simple rest, unable to see why anyone would waste bright days carrying heavy loads. So, he continues to dance and make music under the clear sky, convinced that tomorrow will simply take care of itself.

Many other animals in the field join his game, enjoying the warm weather and forgetting about the future entirely. None of them want to think about the cold season that waits patiently ahead of them. If they had listened to the wise worker, they would have started gathering food immediately. Instead, they waste precious hours on silly games and songs, convinced that worrying is a complete waste of time. The grasshopper points to the sky, asking why anyone would be sad on such a lovely day, and his friends laugh loudly in agreement.

They firmly believe that food will somehow appear miraculously when they need it most. The worker watches them from a distance, feeling a quiet sorrow for their utter lack of vision. He knows that nature is harsh and unforgiving to those who fail to prepare for the inevitable shift in seasons. Yet, the playful creatures continue their daily dance without a single care in the world. The bright summer days pass by very quickly for everyone in the field, until soon the warm breeze is replaced by a chilly wind.

The unmistakable signs of autumn become clear as leaves fall rapidly from the tall trees. Soon, the bright summer ends completely and the cold winter arrives with heavy snow and thick ice. The small grasshopper has no food left anywhere, and he finds himself weak, freezing, and in great trouble. Meanwhile, the worker remains warm inside his snug house with plenty of food to eat. The snow covers the ground completely, and the bitter wind blows hard against the wooden doors.

The grasshopper finally realizes that he made a terrible mistake by ignoring the obvious warning signs. His friends are also suffering immensely from the total lack of food and shelter. They wander aimlessly through the cold white landscape, desperately looking for something to eat. Every place they visit is entirely empty and silent, and the situation becomes more serious with each passing hour. They bitterly wish that they had listened to the advice of the wise worker much earlier in the year.

If the grasshopper had worked during the summer, he would be eating warm food now instead of shivering in the freezing air. His empty stomach causes him great pain as the long night grows darker and more oppressive. The snow falls heavily, covering all the familiar paths they used to walk upon during the warmer months. They try frantically to find berries or seeds beneath the thick ice, but absolutely nothing can be found. Their weak bodies struggle to move through the deep drifts, and the little grasshopper realizes that his playful attitude was entirely wrong.

He remembers how he mocked the worker who was busy storing grain, and now that laughter has turned into bitter tears of regret. The cold wind cuts right through their thin fur, making them feel even worse as the storm intensifies. They huddle together tightly under a large rock to escape the worst of the howling storm. Even in this dark moment, they receive no help from the frozen world around them, learning a harsh lesson from the experience. He realizes now that there is a proper time for work and a distinct time for play.

It is undeniably wise to prepare for the future before hard times begin without warning. Had he worked diligently during the summer, he would not be weak and desperate now. He finally understands the true value of responsibility and proper planning, which changes his view on life completely. He promises himself solemnly that he will never make the exact same mistake again. When the next year comes around, he will be ready to work just like the others.

The worker hears a gentle knock on his heavy door and opens it slowly to see who is there. He sees the cold and hungry animals standing outside in the deep snow, shivering uncontrollably. Although he is naturally disappointed by their past behavior, he feels deep pity for their current plight. He invites them inside his warm house without hesitation and shares his abundant food with them. The grasshopper thanks the kind worker with all his heart, knowing that the lessons of the past will never be forgotten by anyone in the field.
